706	function IndicatorSections({ id }: { id: string }) {
707	  return <>{indicatorExplanations[id].map((lesson, index) => {
708	    const item = translationExamples[id][index];
709	    return <Section key={item.word} title={lesson.title} expressionNumber={index + 1}>
710	      <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote>
711	      {lesson.paragraphs.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
712	      {id === 'group-2' && item.word === 'only if' && <>
713	        <p>Try reading the sentence as though it concerns you. Imagine that you graduate and ask what you now have to know about your project. Then try starting from project completion and see whether the sentence settles graduation.</p>
714	        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Put yourself in the proposed starting condition, then check what the original sentence requires you to know. We're checking its meaning, not predicting what usually happens to graduates.</p>
715	        <ThinkPause prompt="If I graduate, what must be true of my project?">
716	          <p>I've completed it. The sentence requires project completion for anyone who graduates, including me in this imagined case.</p>
717	          <p>Graduates → Completes project matches that reading. Now try the reverse below.</p>
718	        </ThinkPause>
719	      </>}
720	      <ThinkPause prompt={lesson.prompt}>
721	        {lesson.reasoning.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
722	        <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>
723	        <p className="co-readback">{item.readback}</p>
724	      </ThinkPause>
725	      {lesson.after?.map(paragraph => <p key={paragraph}>{paragraph}</p>)}
726	      {lesson.check && <Check {...lesson.check}/>}
727	      {id === 'group-2' && item.word === 'only if' && <DirectionSwitch/>}
728	    </Section>;
729	  })}</>;
730	}
731	
732	function Fundamentals({ roles = false }: { roles?: boolean }) {
733	  return <>
734	    {!roles && <>
735	      <Section title="One condition tells you something else">
736	        <p>A <strong>condition</strong> is something that can be true or false of the situation we're considering. A door is open. A person has a ticket. An order has been packed. Each gives us an idea whose truth we can ask about.</p>
737	        <p>A <strong>conditional statement</strong> connects two conditions. It tells us that if one is true, the other has to be true too. It doesn't tell us that the first condition actually occurred. It tells us what would follow if it did.</p>
738	        <p>That distinction matters. You can understand a rule about people who have tickets without knowing whether anyone has a ticket. Later, a fact about one person may give you a way to use the rule.</p>
739	        <div className="co-intro-relationship"><div><span>One condition is met</span><strong>If this is true...</strong></div><div><span>The statement guarantees another</span><strong>...this has to be true too.</strong></div></div>
740	        <p>You don't need symbols yet. First, understand what the sentence is saying.</p>
741	      </Section>
742	      <Section title="Why learn this?" dark>
743	        <p>On the LSAT, you're often deciding whether a conclusion follows from what you've been told. A conditional may supply the connection you need. It can also show you exactly where an argument asks you to accept more than its information supports.</p>
744	        <p>The important part is the direction. If one condition guarantees another, can we start with the other condition and go back? Another statement might allow that. But this rule alone doesn't.</p>
745	        <p>We'll work with one everyday rule and change what we know about a particular order. You'll see a conclusion the rule supports and another that would need more information. That distinction helps you check what must be true and decide whether an argument has gone too far.</p>
746	      </Section>
747	      <Section title="A guarantee is stronger than a tendency">
748	        <p>Compare these two statements:</p>
749	        <div className="co-intro-relationship"><div><span>A tendency</span><strong>Orders marked ready are usually packed.</strong><p>A ready order could be an exception.</p></div><div><span>A conditional rule</span><strong>Every order marked ready has been packed.</strong><p>A ready order cannot be an exception under this rule.</p></div></div>
750	        <p>Usually leaves room for exceptions. You could find an order marked ready that hasn't been packed and still accept the first statement. The second doesn't leave that possibility open. Every order with that label has been packed.</p>
751	        <p>For an LSAT question, take the stated rule as given. A real shop could make a mistake, but we're asking what follows from this particular statement. The rule gives us a guarantee within the information we're considering.</p>
752	      </Section>
753	      <Section title="Read one rule in ordinary English">
754	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
755	        <p>There are two complete ideas here: an order is <strong>marked ready</strong>, and an order <strong>has been packed</strong>. The sentence tells us something about every order that meets the first condition.</p>
756	        <p>Read it this way: <strong>if an order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> We don't need to know why the shop uses that label. We need to know what the sentence tells us about an order that has it.</p>
757	        <p>The rule itself doesn't identify a particular ready order. A separate fact can tell us whether one of the conditions is met in a particular case.</p>
758	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order A is marked ready. What do we now know about it?">
759	          <p><strong>Order A has been packed.</strong> The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact tells us that Order A is one of those orders. That's the connection. The rule tells us what has to be true of the group; the fact lets us apply it to this case.</p>
760	          <p>We haven't learned that every order in the shop is packed. Our conclusion concerns Order A because that's the order the fact tells us about.</p>
761	        </ThinkPause>
762	      </Section>
763	      <Section title="The arrow is a shorter way to write it">
764	        <p>Now that we understand the sentence, we can give the two conditions shorter labels. The arrow records the connection between them.</p>
765	        <Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/>
766	        <p>Read it from left to right: <strong>if the order is marked ready, then it has been packed.</strong> Knowing the left condition lets us establish the right because every ready order is packed.</p>
767	        <p>The arrow doesn't mean “and then.” It also doesn't claim the label causes the packing. We're recording what follows logically, not a timeline.</p>
768	      </Section>
769	      <Section title="Change the fact, keep the rule">
770	        <p>Keep the rule exactly the same. This time, the fact tells us about packing instead of the ready label.</p>
771	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
772	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order B has been packed. Do we know whether it is marked ready?">
773	          <p><strong>We don't know.</strong> Order B could be marked ready. It could also be packed but not marked ready. Both possibilities fit the statement.</p>
774	          <p>The rule tells us about every ready order. It hasn't told us that every packed order gets that label. To conclude that Order B is ready, we'd need that additional connection.</p>
775	        </ThinkPause>
776	        <p>In the drawing below, ready orders sit inside packed orders because every ready order is packed. A packed order can fit inside or outside the ready group. Use the two facts to compare what each one establishes.</p>
777	        <SetExplorer/>
778	      </Section>
779	      <Section title="Try the same reasoning with a new rule">
780	        <p>You've used the rule in its stated direction and checked what happens when you start from its result. Try that distinction with a different sentence.</p>
781	        <Check prompt="Every volunteer wears a badge. Ari wears a badge. Is Ari necessarily a volunteer?" options={['Yes, the badge proves it.', 'No, Ari could have a badge for another reason.']} correct={1} feedback={['You used the rule backward. Every volunteer has a badge, but the sentence does not say everyone with a badge is a volunteer. A staff member wearing one would fit the rule too.', 'A badge alone leaves volunteer status unknown. The sentence allows a staff member with a badge; it has not restricted badges to volunteers.']}/>
782	        <p>A matching result can make the reverse feel plausible. Check whether the sentence actually gave you that direction before accepting it.</p>
783	        <p>We can now name the two jobs the conditions are doing. One is enough to establish the other. The other is required whenever the first holds.</p>
784	      </Section>
785	    </>}
786	    {roles && <>
787	      <Section title="Sufficient means enough">
788	        <p>An order being marked ready is sufficient for it being packed. It's enough. Once we know the order is marked ready, this rule gives us what we need to conclude it has been packed.</p>
789	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
790	        <p>You don't need a second fact about its contents. The rule already tells you what must be true of every order with that label. We call being marked ready the <strong>sufficient condition</strong>. Here, sufficient means enough to establish packing under this particular rule.</p>
791	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order C is marked ready. Do you need another fact about C to know it has been packed?">
792	          <p>No. The ready label meets the condition the rule tells us about. The rule and that fact are enough to establish packing.</p>
793	          <p>This doesn't make the label the only possible way to find out that an order is packed. It gives us one way to settle that question.</p>
794	        </ThinkPause>
795	      </Section>
796	      <Section title="Enough does not mean the only way">
797	        <p>Suppose Order D isn't marked ready. We no longer have the fact that allowed us to use the rule in its stated direction. But that doesn't settle the packing question.</p>
798	        <ThinkPause prompt="Order D isn't marked ready. Could it still have been packed?">
799	          <p>Yes. D could be packed without a ready label. It could also be unpacked. Neither possibility creates a ready order that hasn't been packed, so both fit the rule.</p>
800	          <p>The sentence gives us one condition that settles packing. It hasn't said packing happens only for those orders. When that sufficient condition isn't present, the other condition may still be true.</p>
801	        </ThinkPause>
802	        <p>Use a new rule to check whether you can recognize what is enough.</p>
803	        <Check prompt="Every delivery marked collected has a pickup receipt. What is sufficient here?" options={['Marked collected', 'Has a pickup receipt']} correct={0} feedback={['Marked collected is enough to establish a pickup receipt. The rule tells us what is true of every delivery with that label.', 'You chose the required result. The sentence gives a receipt to every delivery marked collected; it does not say every delivery with a receipt has that label.']}/>
804	      </Section>
805	      <Section title="Necessary means required">
806	        <p>Now look at the other condition in the same rule. An order marked ready has to have been packed. Being packed is necessary for being marked ready.</p>
807	        <p>We call being packed the <strong>necessary condition</strong>. Necessary means required. It names something that has to be true whenever the sufficient condition is true.</p>
808	        <p>Ask “required for what?” Here, required for an order marked ready. The relationship gives the word its meaning.</p>
809	        <ThinkPause prompt="Which condition does a ready order have to meet?">
810	          <p>It has to be packed. Otherwise we'd have a ready order that wasn't packed, which the sentence rules out.</p>
811	          <p>The ready label is enough to establish packing, and packing is required for every ready order. Those are two views of the same relationship.</p>
812	        </ThinkPause>
813	        <div className="co-role-pair"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Marked ready: enough to establish packing.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Packed: required for every ready order.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>
814	        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between the ready label and packing.</p>
815	        <GateExplorer/>
816	      </Section>
817	      <Section title="Having a requirement does not guarantee the result">
818	        <p>Let's use a different requirement.</p>
819	        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>
820	        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That's why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>
821	        <Arrow left="Enters the archive" right="Has a pass"/>
822	        <ThinkPause prompt="Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?">
823	          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn't tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>
824	          <p>Necessary doesn't mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we'd need information establishing that additional connection.</p>
825	        </ThinkPause>
826	        <Check prompt="Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?" options={['Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.', 'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.']} correct={0} feedback={['The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.', "You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn't a finalist to receive an invitation too."]}/>
827	      </Section>
828	      <Section title="Try the sentence from the inside">
829	        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>
830	        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>
831	        <blockquote>Every order marked ready has been packed.</blockquote>
832	        <p>For this rule, imagine an order of yours. Keep the same order in mind on both sides.</p>
833	        <ThinkPause prompt="If my order is marked ready, what do I know about it?">
834	          <p>It has been packed. Every order marked ready is packed, and my imagined order has that label. Marked ready → Packed matches what the sentence tells me.</p>
835	        </ThinkPause>
836	        <ThinkPause prompt="If my order has been packed, must it be marked ready?">
837	          <p>No. My order could be packed without a ready label. That fits every ready order is packed because it doesn't create a ready order that hasn't been packed.</p>
838	          <p>Packed → Marked ready would add a claim about every packed order. The sentence doesn't give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>
839	        </ThinkPause>
840	      </Section>
841	      <Section title="Read the roles in the relationship">
842	        <p>In <strong>Marked ready → Packed</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it's enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it's required whenever the left holds.</p>
843	        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don't ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>
844	        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We'll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>
845	      </Section>
846	    </>}
847	  </>;
848	}
849	
850	function GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {
851	  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === 'group-1' ? 'g1' : 'g2'))!;
852	  const first = id === 'group-1';
853	  return <>
854	    <Section title={first ? 'Find the sufficient idea' : 'Find what is required'}>
855	      <p>{first
856	        ? "A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. We'll work through the meaning of each expression, then practice finding its complete target."
857	        : "A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, may leave the left condition unknown."}</p>
858	      <div className="co-vocabulary">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>
859	      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. Finding that idea matters more than taking the next few words in the sentence.</p>
860	    </Section>
861	    <IndicatorSections id={id}/>
862	    <Section title={first ? 'Find the target, then place it' : 'Find the requirement, then place it'}>
863	      <p>{first
864	        ? "Add what to the indicator when that gives you a sensible question. If what? All what? The only what? Your answer should be the whole idea the expression refers to. Then check whether that idea really is enough to establish the other condition."
865	        : "Ask what the indicator refers to. Only what? Only if what? What is required? The target can come before or after the expression, so check its meaning in the complete sentence."}</p>
866	      <p>This is the <strong>What Test</strong>. The question helps you find the indicator's complete target before choosing its side of the arrow. Keep any words that limit which people, objects, places, or occasions the sentence covers. The question finds the idea; the whole sentence tells you what that idea does.</p>
867	      <p>Work through the expressions below. First choose the complete target. Then place it on its side of the arrow and read the relationship in ordinary English.</p>
868	      <TranslationLab id={id}/>
869	    </Section>
870	    <Section title="Keep the whole condition" dark>
871	      <blockquote>{first ? 'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.' : 'Entry requires a current pass.'}</blockquote>
872	      <p>{first
873	        ? "Applicant alone isn't the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Keep that restriction when you shorten the idea; otherwise your diagram would make a claim about on-time applicants too."
874	        : "A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. Shortening a label should save writing while preserving what the sentence requires."}</p>
875	      <Check prompt={first ? 'What goes left in this rule?' : 'What goes right in this rule?'} options={first ? ['Applicant', 'Applicant submits late'] : ['Has current pass', 'Has any pass']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first
876	        ? ['You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.', 'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.']
877	        : ['This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.', 'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the sentence requires.']}/>
878	    </Section>
879	    <Section title={first ? 'Read the only as a complete expression' : 'Compare only with the only'}>
880	      {first ? <>
881	        <blockquote>The only people admitted are members.</blockquote>
882	        <p>The only what? The only people admitted. The complete target is admitted people. If someone is admitted, the sentence tells us they're a member.</p>
883	        <Arrow left="Admitted" right="Member"/>
884	        <p>A member who stays outside fits this rule. Nothing says every member is admitted. Check that your diagram keeps that possibility open.</p>
885	        <Check prompt="The only vehicles permitted inside are electric. Which diagram preserves the rule?" options={['Electric → Permitted inside', 'Permitted inside → Electric']} correct={1} feedback={['You made electric status enough for permission. The sentence requires permitted vehicles to be electric; it does not promise admission to every electric vehicle.', 'Permitted vehicles have to be electric. This direction preserves the limit on permitted vehicles without adding a promise about all electric vehicles.']}/>
886	      </> : <>
887	        <p>Try the distinction on the order rule we already understand. The wording changes while the connection stays the same.</p>
888	        <div className="co-word-comparison"><div><blockquote>Only packed orders are marked ready.</blockquote><p>Only refers to packed orders. Packing is required for the ready label, so packed goes right.</p></div><div><blockquote>The only orders marked ready are packed orders.</blockquote><p>The only refers to orders marked ready. That label is enough to establish packing, so marked ready goes left.</p></div></div>
889	        <Arrow left="Marked ready" right="Packed"/>
890	        <p>Both sentences preserve the same direction. An order that is packed but not marked ready fits both. Neither sentence makes packing alone enough for the label.</p>
891	        <Check prompt="Only registered guests receive wristbands. What follows from receiving a wristband?" options={['The person is a registered guest.', 'Nothing about registration.']} correct={0} feedback={['Only limits wristbands to registered guests, so receiving one establishes that the person is registered.', 'You treated the sentence like all registered guests receive wristbands. Only restricts who receives one, so the wristband does establish that the person is a registered guest.']}/>
892	      </>}
893	    </Section>
894	    <Section title="From sentence to diagram">
895	      <p>You have seen the expressions with help. Now use a new sentence. Identify both complete ideas, choose the sufficient one for the left, and check whether your diagram says what the sentence says.</p>
896	      <p>If you know the meaning but hesitate over the arrow, imagine the left condition is true. Does the sentence then require the right condition? Read the whole relationship in English. If it makes a promise the original didn't make, change the direction.</p>
897	      <DiagramBuilder id={id}/>
898	      <details className="co-practical-note"><summary>Why write a diagram when I understand the sentence?</summary><p>For one short sentence, you may be able to hold the connection in your head. Here, writing it makes your direction and complete conditions visible so you can check them. If your first diagram doesn't match the meaning, find the exact phrase you misread and revise that part.</p></details>
899	    </Section>
900	    <Section title="Say the rule without the scaffold">
901	      <p>Read the new sentence and explain its direction before revealing. If you hesitate, ask what the indicator refers to, then try the proposed relationship in ordinary English. Compare your explanation with the one that appears.</p>
902	      <Flashcards id={id}/>
903	    </Section>
904	    <Section title="Check the meaning, not just the word">
905	      <Check prompt={first ? 'A report is reviewed if submitted before noon. Which is the same rule?' : 'A student graduates only if the project is completed. Which is the same rule?'} options={first ? ['If reviewed, it was submitted before noon.', 'If submitted before noon, it is reviewed.'] : ['If the student graduates, the project was completed.', 'If the project is completed, the student graduates.']} correct={first ? 1 : 0} feedback={first
906	        ? ['You reversed the if relationship. Submission before noon is enough for review; the sentence has not said review proves when the report was submitted.', 'This preserves the if-clause as the sufficient condition. Submission before noon gives us review even though it appears second in the original sentence.']
907	        : ['This preserves project completion as a requirement for graduation. It does not turn completing the project into a promise of graduation.', 'You turned the only-if requirement into a guarantee. Graduation requires completion, but the sentence allows a completed project without graduation.']}/>
908	      <p>The guided practice asks you to write your reasoning with the method beside you. Then the attached drill gives you fresh questions. Use the explanation to inspect the direction and complete conditions, not only whether you chose the same answer.</p>
909	    </Section>
910	  </>;
911	}
912	
913	function OpeningFigure({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {
914	  if (unitId === 'orientation') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-meaning-figure">
915	    <p className="co-kicker">A connection between conditions</p>
916	    <div className="co-meaning-premise"><span>One condition</span><strong>If this is true...</strong></div>
917	    <div className="co-meaning-result"><span>Another condition</span><strong>...this has to be true too.</strong></div>
918	    <figcaption>The statement connects them. It doesn't say either one has occurred.</figcaption>
919	  </figure>;
920	  if (unitId === 'sufficient-necessary') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-roles-figure">
921	    <p className="co-kicker">Two views of one rule</p>
922	    <div><span>Enough</span><strong>Marked ready</strong><small>Gives us packing</small></div>
923	    <div><span>Required</span><strong>Packed</strong><small>Every ready order needs it</small></div>
924	    <figcaption>Every order marked ready has been packed.</figcaption>
925	  </figure>;
926	  if (unitId === 'group-1') return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-sufficient-figure">
927	    <p className="co-kicker">Read the complete idea</p>
928	    <p className="co-hero-sentence">A report is reviewed <mark>if</mark> <strong>it is submitted before noon.</strong></p>
929	    <div className="co-hero-target"><span>What is enough for review?</span><strong>Submission before noon</strong></div>
930	    <figcaption>The sufficient condition can come last in the sentence.</figcaption>
931	  </figure>;
932	  return <figure className="co-hero-figure co-required-figure">
933	    <p className="co-kicker">Read what the sentence needs</p>
934	    <p className="co-hero-sentence">Entry requires a <strong>current pass.</strong></p>
935	    <div className="co-pass-figure"><span>Required for entry</span><strong>A current pass</strong><small>An expired pass won't meet this requirement.</small></div>
936	    <figcaption>Having the pass alone doesn't establish entry.</figcaption>
937	  </figure>;
938	}
939	
940	export function ConditionalOpeningLesson({ unit, onComplete, continueHref, continueLabel = 'Continue to guided practice', previousHref, reviewed }: ConditionalLessonNoteProps) {
941	  const [motionPaused, setMotionPaused] = useState(false);
942	  const group = unit.id === 'group-1' || unit.id === 'group-2';
943	  const orientation = unit.id === 'orientation';
944	  const title = group ? unit.id === 'group-1' ? 'Group 1: what is enough?' : 'Group 2: what is required?' : orientation ? 'What Are Conditionals?' : 'What is enough? What is required?';
945	  const repairTitle = orientation ? "Don't turn a rule into a case fact" : unit.id === 'sufficient-necessary' ? "Don't turn a requirement into a guarantee" : unit.id === 'group-1' ? 'Keep the direction and the whole condition' : 'Keep requirements and completed actions distinct';
946	  const subtitle = orientation
947	    ? "A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too. Let's see what that connection lets you conclude, and what it leaves open."
948	    : group ? unit.id === 'group-1'
949	      ? 'These expressions can point to the sufficient idea. Read what each one says before deciding which complete condition belongs on the left.'
950	      : 'These expressions can identify a requirement. Find the complete idea the sentence says you need, then check what its presence does and does not prove.'
951	    : "The same rule gives its two conditions different jobs. Let's take each one seriously before giving it a place in the diagram.";
952	  return <div className="conditional-note co-opening" data-motion-paused={motionPaused}>
953	    <DynamicIslandTOC key={unit.id} selector=".co-opening [data-toc]"/>
954	    <header className={'co-hero ' + (orientation ? 'co-intro-hero' : '')}>
955	      <div className="co-hero-copy"><p className="co-kicker">Conditional logic</p><h1>{title}</h1><p>{subtitle}</p>
956	      <a href="#co-reading">{orientation ? 'Start with the meaning' : 'Begin reading'} <span aria-hidden="true">↓</span></a></div>
957	      <button type="button" className="co-motion-toggle" aria-pressed={motionPaused} onClick={() => setMotionPaused(!motionPaused)}>{motionPaused ? 'Resume motion' : 'Pause motion'}</button>
958	      <OpeningFigure unitId={unit.id}/>
959	    </header>
960	    <article id="co-reading">
961	      {group ? <GroupLessons id={unit.id}/> : <Fundamentals roles={unit.id === 'sufficient-necessary'}/>}
962	      <ConditionalOpeningMedia unitId={unit.id}/>
963	      <Section id="repair" title={repairTitle}>
964	        <div className="co-word-comparison"><div><h3>The tempting move</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.mistake}</p></div><div><h3>Read the sentence again</h3><p>{unit.commonMistake.repair}</p></div></div>
965	      </Section>
966	      <footer className="co-footer">
967	        <p>{reviewed ? 'You marked this lesson as read. You can return to the examples whenever you need.' : 'Marking this lesson as read records review. Your practice gives you a chance to use the reasoning yourself.'}</p>
968	        <div className="co-choices">{previousHref && <Link to={previousHref}>Previous lesson</Link>}<button type="button" onClick={onComplete}>{reviewed ? 'Lesson read' : 'Mark lesson as read'}</button><Link className="co-primary" to={continueHref}>{continueLabel} <span aria-hidden="true">→</span></Link></div>
969	      </footer>
970	    </article>
971	  </div>;
972	}

1	import type { ConditionalDifficulty } from './content/types';

3	export const CONDITIONAL_OPENING_UNIT_IDS = ['orientation', 'sufficient-necessary', 'group-1', 'group-2'] as const;
4	export const CONDITIONAL_OPENING_EDITION = 'opening-2026-10-02-v2';
5	const lessonEditionPrefix = '__lessonEdition:';
6	
7	// Edition timestamps live in the existing lessons map. Stamp them only when the
8	// student marks this edition as read; builds and navigation never change them.
9	export function conditionalOpeningEditionKey(unitId: string): string | undefined {
10	  return CONDITIONAL_OPENING_UNIT_IDS.some(id => id === unitId)
11	    ? `${lessonEditionPrefix}${CONDITIONAL_OPENING_EDITION}:${unitId}` : undefined;
12	}
13	export function isConditionalLessonEditionKey(id: string): boolean {
14	  return id.startsWith(lessonEditionPrefix);
15	}
16	export function needsExpandedConditionalLessonReview(lessons: Record<string, string>, unitId: string): boolean {
17	  const editionKey = conditionalOpeningEditionKey(unitId);
18	  return !!editionKey && !!lessons[unitId] && !lessons[editionKey];
19	}
20

23	  difficulty: ConditionalDifficulty;
24	  previousIds: string[];
25	  addedIds: string[];
26	  replacements: Record<string, string>;
27	}

1	import type { ConditionalDifficulty, ConditionalUnit } from './content/types';
2	import { getCurriculumDifficulty, getCurriculumUnits } from './curriculumPath';
3	import { conditionalOpeningEditionKey, isConditionalLessonEditionKey, openingQuestionRevisions } from './openingRevision';
4	
5	export interface AnswerDraft {
6	  working: string;
7	  hint: boolean;
8	  revealed: boolean;
9	  choice: number | null;
10	  checked: boolean;
11	}
12	export interface ConditionalAttempt {
13	  id: string;
14	  questionId: string;
15	  unitId: string;
16	  difficulty: ConditionalDifficulty;
17	  choice: number | null;
18	  correct: boolean;
19	  hint: boolean;
20	  explanationBeforeCheck: boolean;
21	  working: string;
22	  checkedAt: string;
23	}
24	export interface ConditionalRun {
25	  id: string;
26	  questionIds: string[];
27	  index: number;
28	  drafts: Record<string, AnswerDraft>;
29	  finished: boolean;
30	  elapsed: number;
31	  timerEnabled: boolean;
32	  timerStartedAt?: string | null;
33	}
34	export interface ConditionalProgress {
35	  id: 'conditionals-v1';
36	  lessons: Record<string, string>;
37	  runs: Record<string, ConditionalRun>;
38	  attempts: ConditionalAttempt[];
39	  journal: string;
40	  settings: { showMethod: boolean };
41	  updated_at: string;
42	}
43	export const emptyDraft = (): AnswerDraft => ({ working: '', hint: false, revealed: false, choice: null, checked: false });
44	export const emptyProgress = (): ConditionalProgress => ({ id: 'conditionals-v1', lessons: {}, runs: {}, attempts: [], journal: '', settings: { showMethod: true }, updated_at: new Date().toISOString() });
45	export function markConditionalLessonRead(progress: ConditionalProgress, unitId: string, readAt = new Date().toISOString()): ConditionalProgress {
46	  const editionKey = conditionalOpeningEditionKey(unitId);
47	  return { ...progress, lessons: { ...progress.lessons, [unitId]: readAt, ...(editionKey ? { [editionKey]: readAt } : {}) } };
48	}
49	export function reviewedConditionalLessonCount(lessons: Record<string, string>): number {
50	  return Object.keys(lessons).filter(id => !isConditionalLessonEditionKey(id)).length;
51	}
52	export function newRun(questionIds: string[]): ConditionalRun {
53	  return { id: crypto.randomUUID(), questionIds, index: 0, drafts: {}, finished: false, elapsed: 0, timerEnabled: false };
54	}
55	function hasCheckedQuestion(run: ConditionalRun, questionId: string, attempts: ConditionalAttempt[]): boolean {
56	  return !!run.drafts[questionId]?.checked || attempts.some(attempt => attempt.questionId === questionId && attempt.id.startsWith(`${run.id}:`));
57	}
58	export function nextUncheckedQuestionIndex(run: ConditionalRun, attempts: ConditionalAttempt[], after = -1): number {
59	  const pending = (id: string) => !hasCheckedQuestion(run, id, attempts);
60	  const next = run.questionIds.findIndex((id, index) => index > after && pending(id));
61	  return next >= 0 ? next : run.questionIds.findIndex(pending);
62	}
63	export function reconcileConditionalRun(run: ConditionalRun, questions: { id: string; unitId: string; difficulty: ConditionalDifficulty }[], attempts: ConditionalAttempt[]): ConditionalRun {
64	  const savedIds = new Set(run.questionIds);
65	  const removedIds = new Set<string>();
66	  const addedIds: string[] = [];
67	  for (const revision of openingQuestionRevisions) {
68	    if (![...revision.previousIds, ...revision.addedIds].some(id => savedIds.has(id))) continue;
69	    const allowedIds = new Set(questions.filter(question => question.unitId === revision.unitId && question.difficulty === revision.difficulty).map(question => question.id));
70	    for (const [retiredId, replacementId] of Object.entries(revision.replacements)) {
71	      if (savedIds.has(retiredId) && allowedIds.has(replacementId)) removedIds.add(retiredId);
72	    }
73	    for (const id of revision.addedIds) if (allowedIds.has(id) && !savedIds.has(id)) addedIds.push(id);
74	  }
75	  if (!removedIds.size && !addedIds.length) return run;
76	  const questionIds = [...run.questionIds.filter(id => !removedIds.has(id)), ...addedIds];
77	  const currentId = run.questionIds[run.index];
78	  const currentIndex = questionIds.indexOf(currentId);
79	  const keepCurrent = !run.finished && currentIndex >= 0 && questions.some(question => question.id === currentId);
80	  const updated = { ...run, questionIds };
81	  const pendingIndex = nextUncheckedQuestionIndex(updated, attempts);
82	  const index = keepCurrent ? currentIndex : pendingIndex >= 0 ? pendingIndex : questionIds.length;
83	  return { ...updated, index, finished: !keepCurrent && pendingIndex < 0 };
84	}
85	export function attemptSummary(attempts: ConditionalAttempt[]) {
86	  return { total: attempts.length, correct: attempts.filter(a => a.correct).length, assisted: attempts.filter(a => a.hint || a.explanationBeforeCheck).length };
87	}
88	export function parseConditionalProgress(value: unknown): ConditionalProgress {
89	  const bad = () => { throw new Error('Your stored conditional progress has an unsupported format. Your record has been preserved; ask Joshua to review it.'); };
90	  if (!value || typeof value !== 'object') return bad();
91	  const record = value as ConditionalProgress;
92	  if (record.id !== 'conditionals-v1' || !record.lessons || typeof record.lessons !== 'object' || !record.runs || typeof record.runs !== 'object' || !Array.isArray(record.attempts) || typeof record.journal !== 'string' || typeof record.settings?.showMethod !== 'boolean' || !Number.isFinite(Date.parse(record.updated_at))) return bad();
93	  if (Object.values(record.lessons).some(date => typeof date !== 'string' || !Number.isFinite(Date.parse(date)))) return bad();
94	  for (const run of Object.values(record.runs)) {
95	    if (!run || typeof run.id !== 'string' || !Array.isArray(run.questionIds) || run.questionIds.some(id => typeof id !== 'string') || !Number.isInteger(run.index) || run.index < 0 || run.index > run.questionIds.length || !run.drafts || typeof run.drafts !== 'object' || typeof run.finished !== 'boolean' || typeof run.timerEnabled !== 'boolean' || !Number.isFinite(run.elapsed) || run.elapsed < 0 || (run.timerStartedAt != null && !Number.isFinite(Date.parse(run.timerStartedAt)))) return bad();
96	    for (const draft of Object.values(run.drafts)) if (!draft || typeof draft.working !== 'string' || typeof draft.hint !== 'boolean' || typeof draft.revealed !== 'boolean' || typeof draft.checked !== 'boolean' || (draft.choice !== null && (!Number.isInteger(draft.choice) || draft.choice < 0))) return bad();
97	  }
98	  for (const attempt of record.attempts) if (!attempt || typeof attempt.id !== 'string' || typeof attempt.questionId !== 'string' || typeof attempt.unitId !== 'string' || !['beginner', 'medium', 'advanced'].includes(attempt.difficulty) || typeof attempt.correct !== 'boolean' || typeof attempt.hint !== 'boolean' || typeof attempt.explanationBeforeCheck !== 'boolean' || typeof attempt.working !== 'string' || !Number.isFinite(Date.parse(attempt.checkedAt)) || (attempt.choice !== null && (!Number.isInteger(attempt.choice) || attempt.choice < 0))) return bad();
99	  return record;
100	}
101	export function elapsedSeconds(run: ConditionalRun, now = Date.now()): number {
102	  return run.elapsed + (run.timerStartedAt ? Math.max(0, Math.floor((now - Date.parse(run.timerStartedAt)) / 1000)) : 0);
103	}
104	export function recommendNext(progress: ConditionalProgress, units: ConditionalUnit[], startingUnitId?: string) {
105	  for (const unit of getCurriculumUnits(units, startingUnitId)) {
106	    const difficulty = getCurriculumDifficulty(unit);
107	    const phase = unit.phase;
108	    if (!progress.lessons[unit.id]) return { mode: 'learn' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Learn: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };
109	    if (!progress.attempts.some(attempt => attempt.questionId === unit.guidedExample.id)) return { mode: 'learn' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Guided example: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };
110	    if (unit.drills[difficulty].some(question => !progress.attempts.some(attempt => attempt.questionId === question.id && attempt.difficulty === difficulty))) {
111	      return { mode: 'practice' as const, unitId: unit.id, label: `Skill drill: ${unit.title}`, difficulty, phase };
112	    }
113	  }
114	  return null;
115	}

226	function nextAvailableQuestionIndex(run: ConditionalRun, questions: PracticeQuestion[], attempts: ConditionalProgress['attempts'], after = -1): number {
227	  const availableIds = new Set(questions.map(question => question.id));
228	  const questionIds = run.questionIds.filter(id => availableIds.has(id));
229	  const visibleAfter = questionIds.filter(id => run.questionIds.indexOf(id) <= after).length - 1;
230	  const index = nextUncheckedQuestionIndex({ ...run, questionIds }, attempts, visibleAfter);
231	  return index < 0 ? -1 : run.questionIds.indexOf(questionIds[index]);
232	}

247	      return nextRun === savedRun ? previous : { ...previous, runs: { ...previous.runs, [activeRunKey]: nextRun } };
248	    });
249	  }, [run, activeRunKey, questions, progress.attempts, update]);

251	  const startAssignmentSet = () => update(previous => {
252	    const priorAssignmentRun = previous.runs[assignmentRunKey];
253	    return { ...previous, runs: { ...previous.runs,
254	      ...(priorAssignmentRun ? { [`${assignmentRunKey}:history:${priorAssignmentRun.id}`]: priorAssignmentRun } : {}),
255	      [assignmentRunKey]: newRun(questions.map(question => question.id)) } };
256	  });
257	  const timerStartedAt = run?.timerStartedAt;
258	  useEffect(() => {
259	    if (!timerStartedAt) return;
260	    const timer = setInterval(() => setTimerNow(Date.now()), 1000);
261	    return () => clearInterval(timer);
262	  }, [timerStartedAt]);
263	  if (!run) return <p>Preparing your practice...</p>;
264	  if (reconcileConditionalRun(run, questions, progress.attempts) !== run) return <p>Preparing your practice...</p>;
265	  const availableIds = new Set(questions.map(question => question.id));
266	  const visibleIds = run.questionIds.filter(id => availableIds.has(id));
267	  const currentIndex = run.finished ? -1 : availableIds.has(run.questionIds[run.index]) ? run.index : nextAvailableQuestionIndex(run, questions, progress.attempts);
268	  const question = questions.find(item => item.id === run.questionIds[currentIndex]);
269	  const activeIds = new Set(visibleIds);
270	  const attempts = progress.attempts.filter(item => item.id.startsWith(`${run.id}:`) && activeIds.has(item.questionId));
271	  const expandedLessonReview = !guidedMethod && <ExpandedLessonReview questions={questions} selection={forwardSelection ?? selection} />;
272	  if (!question) {
273	    const summary = attemptSummary(attempts);
274	    return <>{expandedLessonReview}<h2>{visibleIds.length ? 'You finished this set.' : 'This assignment has a different practice set.'}</h2><p>{summary.correct} correct out of {summary.total} checked answers. {summary.assisted} used a hint or explanation before checking.</p><p>Revisit the reasoning, then continue with the next activity. Finishing a set does not certify mastery. Your previous written work and attempts stay saved when you start another set.</p><div className="cb-actions"><Link className="cb-button" to={`${BASE}/review${pathUrl(forwardSelection ?? pathSelection(new URLSearchParams())).replace(BASE, '')}`}>Review your attempts</Link><button onClick={startAssignmentSet}><RotateCcw size={16} /> {assignmentId ? "Start this assignment's set" : 'Repeat this set'}</button></div><RecommendedForward selection={forwardSelection} /></>;
275	  }
276	  const draft = run.drafts[question.id] ?? emptyDraft();
277	  const nextIndex = nextAvailableQuestionIndex(run, questions, progress.attempts, currentIndex);
278	  const advance = () => update(previous => {
279	    const currentRun = previous.runs[activeRunKey];
280	    const nextAvailable = nextAvailableQuestionIndex(currentRun, questions, previous.attempts, currentIndex);
281	    const index = nextAvailable >= 0 ? nextAvailable : nextUncheckedQuestionIndex(currentRun, previous.attempts);
282	    return { ...previous, runs: { ...previous.runs, [activeRunKey]: { ...currentRun, index: index < 0 ? currentRun.questionIds.length : index, finished: index < 0, elapsed: elapsedSeconds(currentRun), timerStartedAt: currentRun.timerStartedAt && nextAvailable >= 0 ? new Date().toISOString() : null } } };
283	  });
284	  const changeDraft = (patch: Partial<AnswerDraft>) => replaceRun(value => ({ ...value, index: currentIndex, drafts: { ...value.drafts, [question.id]: { ...(value.drafts[question.id] ?? emptyDraft()), ...patch } } }));
285	  const checkAnswer = () => update(previous => {

28	    id: 'orientation', title: "What Are Conditionals?", area: 'understanding', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: [],
29	    goal: "Explain what a conditional says, use it with a fact, and say what the information leaves open.",
30	    teaching: [
31	      "A conditional tells us that if one condition is true, another has to be true too. A condition is something we can say about a situation, such as an order being marked ready or its having been packed. The conditional connects those two ideas. It doesn't tell us that either one has happened.",
32	      "Why learn this? A conclusion on the LSAT can depend on which way that connection runs. If you know what the rule promises, you can explain why a conclusion follows. If an answer uses the rule in the other direction, you can see the missing connection instead of accepting a conclusion that sounds reasonable.",
33	      "Let's use one rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. Take that claim as true for this exercise. Every ready order has to be packed. Orders marked ready are usually packed would say something weaker, because usually leaves room for an exception. Our rule doesn't.",
34	      "Now we get a fact: Order A is marked ready. What do you know about A? Take a moment to explain your answer before reading on. A has been packed. The rule covers every order marked ready, and the fact puts A in that group. A ready order that hadn't been packed would break the rule.",
35	      "Keep the rule and change the fact: Order B has been packed. Must B be marked ready? Try to explain why before reading on. We don't know. The rule told us about ready orders. It didn't say every packed order is marked ready. B could be marked ready or packed without being marked ready. Either possibility fits what we've been told.",
36	      "We can now shorten the relationship to Marked ready → Packed. Read that as: if an order is marked ready, it has been packed. The arrow records the connection we've already understood. It doesn't add a cause, a time sequence, or a fact about a particular order.",
37	      "In the practice, separate the rule from the fact about the case. Then ask what that fact lets you conclude. Write the reason as well as the answer. That gives you something specific to compare with the explanation if your choice goes wrong.",
38	    ],
39	    method: [
40	      "Read the rule in ordinary English: what does it say has to be true if the first condition is true?",
41	      "Check the strength. Every ready order is packed gives a guarantee; usually packed allows an exception.",
42	      "Find the separate fact about the case. A rule alone doesn't identify a ready order.",
43	      "Compare the whole fact with the condition the rule covers. Order A is marked ready meets our ready-order condition.",
44	      "State what follows and why. If the information leaves a question open, show how both possibilities could fit.",

105	    id: 'sufficient-necessary', title: "What is enough? What is required?", area: 'understanding', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: ["orientation"],
106	    goal: "Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required, then check which cases fit the same rule.",
107	    teaching: [
108	      "Return to our rule: Every order marked ready has been packed. If you know an order is marked ready, that's enough to know it has been packed. You don't need another fact about that order. Being marked ready is the sufficient condition. Sufficient means enough, here enough to establish packing under this rule.",
109	      "Packed is the necessary condition. Necessary means required. A ready order has to have been packed, because an unpacked ready order would be an exception to our rule. So the same relationship has two sides: marked ready is enough to establish packed, and packed is required for marked ready.",
110	      "Does required mean enough? Suppose an order is packed. Must it be marked ready? We still don't know. Packing meets a requirement for ready status, but the rule hasn't said it settles that status. An order that's packed but not marked ready would fit the rule.",
111	      "Now suppose an order isn't marked ready. Does that settle whether it's packed? No. Our rule tells us what ready orders must have. An order without that label could be packed or unpacked. We haven't been given a fact that decides between them.",
112	      "Compare all four combinations under this one rule. Ready and packed fits. Not ready and packed fits too. Not ready and not packed also fits. Ready and not packed breaks the guarantee. A case fitting the rule means it is possible under that rule, not that the rule tells us it occurred.",
113	      "The names belong to this relationship. Packed is necessary for ready status here. In a different rule, such as every packed order has a tracking number, packed would be sufficient for that tracking number. Ask enough for what, or required for what, rather than giving an idea one permanent label.",
114	      "When a sentence feels unclear, try a Personalized If/Then. Read it naturally first. Then imagine you meet the condition you think is sufficient. What does the sentence force you to know? Check that thought against the whole sentence, not just against a feeling that the arrow looks right.",
115	      "A recovery code is required for a verified account. If my account is verified, what must it have? A recovery code. Now try the other direction: if I have a recovery code, must my account be verified? The sentence hasn't promised that. This check helps us hear why the requirement belongs on the right.",
116	    ],
117	    method: [
118	      "Read the whole sentence in ordinary English before deciding which condition is enough.",
119	      "Ask enough for what. Name both ideas completely: marked ready is enough to establish packed under our rule.",
120	      "Ask required for what. Packed is required for marked ready; that doesn't make packed enough to establish ready status.",
121	      "Check your interpretation with a Personalized If/Then. Try the proposed starting condition, then compare the reverse with the original sentence.",
122	      "Test a possible case against the actual guarantee. Keep a case that fits separate from a case the information establishes.",
123	    ],

126	      reasoning: ["Packing is required for ready status. The rule hasn't promised ready status for every packed order.", "C can therefore be packed without a ready label. That case fits the rule; it doesn't supply a reverse rule."],
127	      conclusion: "Packed without marked ready fits this rule.",
128	    },

150	    id: 'truth-validity', title: 'Does the Conclusion Have to Follow?', area: 'understanding', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: ['rules-facts'],
151	    goal: 'Test whether true premises could coexist with a false conclusion.',
152	    teaching: [
153	      'Truth concerns whether a statement matches the facts. Validity concerns the connection between premises and conclusion. To test a deduction, temporarily accept its premises. Ask whether its conclusion could still be false.',
154	      'Every copper key is magnetic. This key is copper. Therefore this key is magnetic. The reasoning follows the given rule, even if the first premise is false outside the exercise. We are testing the inference, not endorsing the premise.',
155	      'A counterexample keeps every premise true while making the conclusion false. For A → B and B, therefore A, a case with B but no A does that. Invalid does not mean the conclusion must be false. It means the premises do not establish it.',
156	    ],
157	    method: ['Accept the supplied premises for the inference test.', 'Identify the exact conclusion.', 'Try to keep all premises true while making that conclusion false.', 'If such a case is possible, the conclusion is not guaranteed.'],
158	    workedExample: { statements: ['Every circular tile is blue.', 'Tile P is blue.', 'Claim: Tile P is circular.'], reasoning: ['The rule allows a blue square tile.', 'That case preserves the premises and defeats the claim.'], conclusion: 'Circularity is not guaranteed.' },
159	    guidedExample: question('truth-validity-guide', 'Every enrolled swimmer has a locker. Ren is enrolled as a swimmer. Therefore Ren has a locker. The inference is:', ['Valid from the premises.', 'Invalid because lockers may be expensive.', 'Invalid because enrollment is not necessary for a locker.'], 0, 'Only test the direction actually used.', ['Ren meets the rule\'s sufficient condition.', 'The conclusion is the required result, so it follows.']),
160	    commonMistake: { mistake: 'Rejecting an inference because a premise sounds unrealistic.', repair: 'First assess whether the conclusion follows if the premises are true. Assess premise truth as a separate question.' },
161	    drills: {

Does the conclusion follow from these premises?', ['Yes.', 'No, because gold is not normally transparent.', 'Only if transparent leaves are gold.'], 0, 'Treat the unusual premise as given.', ['R satisfies the gold condition.', 'Transparency follows by the stated rule regardless of real-world plausibility.']),
164	        question('truth-validity-b2', 'Every bound booklet has a cover. Booklet D has a cover. Therefore D is bound. The problem is:', ['The conclusion contradicts the rule.', 'The rule was reversed without support.', 'The premises establish no cover.'], 1, 'A cover may have another source.', ['The premises allow an unbound covered booklet.', 'They therefore do not guarantee binding.']),
165	        question('truth-validity-b3', 'An invalid argument\'s conclusion must be:', ['False.', 'True.', 'Neither; validity alone does not settle its truth.'], 2, 'Failure of proof is not proof of falsity.', ['Invalidity means the premises do not force the conclusion.', 'The conclusion may still be true for other reasons.']),
166	      ],

240	  },
241	  {
242	    id: 'group-1', title: "Group 1: what is enough?", area: 'translation', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: ["sufficient-necessary"],
243	    goal: "Find the complete sufficient idea in each Group 1 expression and preserve its meaning in a diagram.",
244	    teaching: [
245	      "We've understood enough and required. Now let's see how different words express the sufficient side. Group 1 includes if, when, where, all, the only, every and any in their conditional uses. The words help you find a relationship. You still need to read what the whole sentence says.",
246	      "Start with if: If an order is marked ready, it has been packed. If what? The order is marked ready. That's the sufficient idea. Now move the clause: An order has been packed if it is marked ready. The same idea still follows if, and the same guarantee runs from marked ready to packed. The first words on the page aren't always the left side of the diagram.",
247	      "When can introduce the event that's enough: When the alarm sounds, the staff evacuate. Ask when what. The alarm sounds. The sentence promises evacuation whenever the alarm sounds. Whenever makes the same repeated-case meaning explicit. But When I checked the desk, a notice was there reports one occasion; it doesn't promise a notice every time I check.",
248	      "Where can make a rule about a location or situation: Where a station is available, the service operates. Which locations does the rule cover? Locations with an available station. At one of those locations, the service has to operate. Keep the location connection when you read the rule back; a station elsewhere hasn't met this location's condition.",
249	      "All covers the whole group it names: All registered guests receive a wristband. All what? Registered guests, not guests generally. Being one of those registered guests is enough for a wristband. Another guest may receive one too; the sentence hasn't told us. It also hasn't told us that a registered guest exists.",
250	      "The only needs careful reading: The only vehicles permitted inside are electric vehicles. The only what? Vehicles permitted inside. If a vehicle is permitted inside, it must be electric. Try electric vehicle → permitted inside. That would grant permission to every electric vehicle, which the sentence never promised. The grammatical target is the whole permission description, not the word electric.",
251	      "Every takes the members one at a time but covers them all: Every finalist receives an invitation. Choose a finalist. The sentence promises that person an invitation. All finalists receive an invitation would express the same relationship. Neither wording says everyone receiving an invitation is a finalist.",
252	      "Any says that whichever member of the named group you choose, the rule applies: Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. A late-submitting applicant pays a fee. That's what the sentence guarantees. Choose an applicant who submits on time and this sentence doesn't settle the fee question. Submits late stays with applicant in your diagram.",
253	      "The What Test keeps you with the complete idea: if what, when what, where what, all what, the only what, every what, or any what? Don't stop at the nearest noun. In A report appears when a rover crosses the ridge, the target is the rover crossing the ridge. That event goes left, even though the sentence begins with the report.",
254	      "An indicator can have another job. The curator asked if the painting was insured tells us about a question the curator asked. It gives no guarantee between asking and insurance. Read for a conditional meaning before drawing an arrow.",
255	      "To build the diagram, name both complete ideas and give them clear condensed labels. Place the sufficient idea on the left and what it guarantees on the right. Then read the arrow as an if/then. Does that sentence say the same thing as the original? If a label lost a restriction or the arrow added a reverse, repair it before moving on.",
256	      "For Recall and Rephrase, put the list aside and try a fresh sentence. Write its if/then meaning, then compare with the original. The point is to keep the relationship when the wording changes, not merely to remember which side a word usually points toward.",
257	    ],
258	    method: [
259	      "Read the sentence for a guarantee, then identify the Group 1 expression. Use the What Test to find the whole idea it refers to.",
260	      "Name both complete ideas. Keep who or what they concern, as well as restrictions such as registered or submits late.",
261	      "Choose clear condensed labels for those ideas. A shorter label must still mean the same thing.",
262	      "Place the sufficient idea left and what it guarantees right. Sentence order doesn't decide the arrow's direction.",
263	      "Read the diagram as an if/then and compare it with the original. Check the direction and every restriction you kept in the labels.",
264	    ],

304	  {
305	    id: 'group-2', title: "Group 2: what is required?", area: 'translation', phase: 'conditional', prerequisites: ["group-1"],
306	    goal: "Find what a Group 2 sentence requires, preserve its complete meaning, and distinguish a requirement from success or compliance.",
307	    teaching: [
308	      "Group 2 helps us find the necessary side: what the sentence says is required. Its core expressions are only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires and must in their relevant uses. We'll keep the complete requirement on the right. First, let's hear why it belongs there.",
309	      "Compare if with only if. An order is marked ready if it has been packed makes packing enough for ready status. An order is marked ready only if it has been packed says packing is required for ready status. Those aren't the same rule. In the second sentence, an order could be packed without being marked ready. The sentence permits that possibility.",
310	      "Only restricts who or what can have the other condition: Only packed orders are marked ready. If an order is marked ready, it must be packed. Only what? Packed orders. Compare The only orders marked ready are packed orders. That Group 1 form reaches the same ready → packed relationship, but its target is orders marked ready. Read the phrase with its grammar rather than treating only and the only as interchangeable labels.",
311	      "Only if introduces a condition that has to be met: A student graduates only if the student completes the project. If the student graduates, the project must be completed. Project completion by itself doesn't promise graduation. Read the reverse aloud and you'll hear the extra guarantee it adds.",
312	      "Only when makes an event or state required: A review occurs only when a complaint is filed. A complaint has to be filed for this review to occur. Imagine a complaint has been filed. Does this sentence say a review now occurs? No. It says what a review requires, not that every complaint gets one.",
313	      "Only where makes a location condition required: The service operates only where a station is available. Wherever the service operates, a station has to be available there. A station at another location isn't enough. Nor has the sentence promised service to every place with an available station.",
314	      "Always describes the result that holds throughout the named cases: A finalist always receives an invitation. Start with a finalist. Under that claim, the person receives an invitation. The subject supplies the sufficient idea; receiving an invitation is the required result. Always belongs in this teaching group, but it doesn't mean the next word automatically goes on the right. Read the complete subject and result.",
315	      "Requires tells you what is needed: Entry requires a current pass. Requires what? A current pass. Entry goes left, having the current pass goes right. Change the wording to A current pass is required for entry. The requirement appears first now, but the relationship hasn't changed. A pass alone isn't a promise that you can enter, and an expired pass doesn't meet the full requirement.",
316	      "Must can state an obligation: A finalist must attend the interview. What does finalist status establish? The person is required to attend. It doesn't establish that the person actually attends; people can fail to meet an obligation. Write Finalist → Required to attend interview so the diagram preserves that difference. Keep permission words such as may in the same careful way.",
317	      "Use the What Test on the full expression, then check with a Personalized If/Then. If my display has activated, what does the battery rule force me to know? Its battery is charged. Now suppose I know only that the battery is charged. The rule still leaves activation open. That comparison checks the arrow against the sentence's ordinary meaning.",
318	      "Keep every part of a requirement. In Entry requires a current pass, pass would drop current. In Only members may use this room, actually uses the room would change permission into an action. The right direction can't repair a label that has lost part of the idea.",
319	      "In the fresh practice, explain what is required and what remains open. If your answer treats a requirement as enough, try saying that answer as an if/then. Compare it with the original sentence and point to the extra promise. That's the mistake we're learning to catch.",
320	    ],
321	    method: [
322	      "Read the whole conditional claim. Identify the Group 2 expression and ask what it requires or restricts with the What Test.",
323	      "Name both complete ideas. Keep restrictions, negatives, permission and obligation in their meaning.",
324	      "Choose clear condensed labels. Required to attend must not become actually attends.",
325	      "Place the restricted event or condition left and its requirement right. Check that direction with a Personalized If/Then.",
326	      "Read the arrow back in English. Compare the reverse too: did the original sentence really make the requirement enough?",
327	    ],
328	    workedExample: {
329	      statements: ["A package is released only when its clearance is recorded."],
330	      reasoning: ["Only when what? Its clearance is recorded. A released package has to meet that requirement, so release goes left and recorded clearance goes right.", "Read it back: if a package is released, its clearance is recorded. Recorded clearance alone doesn't promise release; the sentence hasn't given us that direction."],
331	      conclusion: "Package released → its clearance recorded.",
332	    },
333	    guidedExample: question("group-2-guide", "Only subscribers can access the recording. Before choosing, identify what only restricts, name both complete ideas, put the requirement right, and check your direction with a Personalized If/Then about having access permission. Which follows?", ["Subscriber → can access recording.", "Can access recording → subscriber.", "Subscriber → has accessed recording."], 1, "If I can access the recording, what does only subscribers require about me?", ["Can access recording → subscriber preserves the restriction: someone with access permission has to be a subscriber.", "The sentence doesn't say every subscriber can access the recording. Nor does permission tell us someone actually accessed it."]),
334	    commonMistake: { mistake: "Taking a requirement as a guarantee of the result, or taking an obligation as proof that someone complied.", repair: "Ask exactly what was promised. Having a current pass meets the requirement for entry, but it doesn't establish that someone entered. Must attend establishes required attendance, not actual attendance. Keep that distinction in the diagram and conclusion." },

8	    { question: 'What does a conditional statement tell you?', answer: 'If one condition is true, the other has to be true too. The statement gives you that connection; it does not establish that either condition actually occurs. A matching fact can let you use the rule for a particular case.' },
9	    { question: 'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 12 is marked reserved. What do we know?', answer: 'Locker 12 has a name card. The rule covers every reserved locker, and the fact tells us Locker 12 belongs to that group. That matching condition is why the conclusion follows.' },
10	    { question: 'Every locker marked reserved has a name card. Locker 9 has a name card. Is it necessarily marked reserved?', answer: 'No. The sentence tells us what every reserved locker has. It has not restricted name cards to reserved lockers. Locker 9 could be reserved or unreserved; its name card alone leaves that question open.' },
11	    { question: 'Why do conditionals matter when evaluating an argument?', answer: 'They can supply the connection that makes a conclusion follow. They can also show where an author needs more information. If the argument uses a rule backward, check whether anything actually gave it that additional direction.' },
12	  ],
13	  'sufficient-necessary': [
14	    { question: 'What does sufficient mean?', answer: 'Enough to establish the other condition under this rule. Once the sufficient condition is met, the rule gives you the necessary condition. Enough does not mean it is the only possible way for the other condition to be true.' },
15	    { question: 'Every order marked ready has been packed. Does packing establish the ready label?', answer: "No. Every ready order has to be packed, but the sentence doesn't say every packed order is marked ready. A packed order without a ready label fits the rule. We need more information to settle its ready status." },
16	    { question: 'What does necessary mean?', answer: 'Required whenever the sufficient condition holds. Ask required for what, and read the complete relationship. In Marked ready → Packed, packing is required for every ready order; those roles belong to this particular rule.' },
17	    { question: 'If a requirement is met, does that prove the other condition happened?', answer: 'No. A requirement may be present while the other condition remains unknown. Entry requires a pass, but a person could have a pass and stay outside. The rule has not turned the pass into a guarantee of entry.' },
18	  ],
19	  'group-1': [
20	    { question: 'A student receives feedback if the student revises the essay. Write the rule.', answer: 'Revises essay → Receives feedback. If refers to revising, even though that clause appears last. The sentence gives feedback to each student who revises. Feedback alone does not establish that the student revised.' },
21	    { question: 'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. Why is “Applicant → Pays fee” too broad?', answer: 'It drops the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would include someone who submitted on time, whom the sentence does not cover. Keep the complete rule: Applicant submits late → Pays fee.' },
22	    { question: 'Every student who registers early receives a discount. Write the rule.', answer: 'Student registers early → Receives discount. Every refers to students who register early. Early registration is part of the sufficient condition, so keep it when you shorten the idea.' },
23	    { question: 'A fee is paid by any applicant who submits late. Write the rule.', answer: 'Applicant submits late → Pays fee. Any still refers to late-submitting applicants. Moving that phrase later does not change its sufficient role. Read the arrow as: if an applicant submits late, that applicant pays a fee.' },
24	  ],
25	  'group-2': [
26	    { question: 'A permit is required for construction. Write the rule.', answer: 'Construction → Has permit. What is required? The permit, even though it appears before required. The sentence makes it necessary for construction, not enough to establish that construction occurs.' },
27	    { question: 'Why not automatically take the words immediately after the indicator?', answer: 'The complete target can appear before or after the expression. In a permit is required for construction, the permit is the requirement. Ask what the indicator refers to, then check your diagram against the meaning of the whole sentence.' },
28	    { question: 'A finalist must attend the interview. Maya attended. Does that prove she is a finalist?', answer: 'No. The rule gives finalists an obligation; it does not say only finalists attend. Actual attendance is also different from being required to attend. The fact about Maya does not establish finalist status.' },
29	    { question: 'A garden light switches on only when its sensor detects darkness. Write the rule.', answer: 'Light switches on → Sensor detects darkness. Only when makes darkness detection required for switching on. The sentence has not said detection is enough, so knowing the sensor detects darkness alone would leave the light unknown.' },
30	  ],
31	};

33	function Recall({ items }: { items: Card[] }) {
34	  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
35	  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false);
36	  const answerId = useId();
37	  const item = items[index];
38	  return <div className="co-flash">
39	    <p className="co-kicker">Review · {index + 1} of {items.length}</p>
40	    <div className={`co-flash-face ${revealed ? 'co-flipped' : ''}`}>
41	      <strong>{item.question}</strong><span>Answer in your own words, then compare.</span>
42	      <div id={answerId} aria-live="polite">{revealed && <p>{item.answer}</p>}</div>
43	    </div>
44	    <div className="co-choices">
45	      <button type="button" aria-expanded={revealed} aria-controls={answerId} onClick={() => setRevealed(!revealed)}>{revealed ? 'Hide recall explanation' : 'Reveal recall explanation'}</button>
46	      <button type="button" disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setRevealed(false); }}>Previous example</button>
47	      <button type="button" onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setRevealed(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? 'Start these again' : 'Next example'}</button>
48	    </div>
49	  </div>;
50	}
51	
52	export function ConditionalOpeningMedia({ unitId }: { unitId: string }) {
53	  // Group lessons already have seven original sentence-to-rule recall cards.
54	  // Their final meaning check closes the lesson without repeating a second deck.
55	  if (unitId === 'group-1' || unitId === 'group-2') return null;
56	  const items = cards[unitId];
57	  if (!items) return null;
58	  return <section id="co-return-to-the-relationship" className="co-section" data-toc data-toc-title="Return to the relationship">
59	    <h2>Return to the relationship</h2>
60	    <p>Try these before moving on. Say what the rule gives you and explain why.</p>
61	    <Recall key={unitId} items={items}/>
62	    {unitId === 'sufficient-necessary' && <details className="co-media-summary">
63	      <summary>See the relationships together</summary>
64	      <figure className="co-relationship-overview">
65	        <h3>Every order marked ready has been packed.</h3>
66	        <div className="co-overview-roles"><div><strong>Marked ready</strong><span>Sufficient: enough</span></div><b aria-hidden="true">→</b><span className="co-sr-only">implies</span><div><strong>Packed</strong><span>Necessary: required</span></div></div>
67	        <div className="co-overview-set">
68	          <svg viewBox="0 0 600 250" role="img" aria-labelledby="co-order-overview-title co-order-overview-desc">
69	            <title id="co-order-overview-title">Marked ready is sufficient. Packed is necessary.</title>
70	            <desc id="co-order-overview-desc">The ready group is contained within packed orders. All ready orders are packed. Packed alone does not establish the ready label. The drawing does not require unequal group sizes.</desc>
71	            <rect x="10" y="10" width="580" height="230" rx="24" fill="#d7e9df"/>
72	            <rect x="40" y="78" width="285" height="132" rx="18" fill="#fcf3df" stroke="#bd8a47"/>
73	          </svg>
74	          <span className="co-overview-packed-label">Packed orders</span><span className="co-overview-ready-label">Orders marked ready</span>
75	        </div>
76	        <p>Packed alone: the ready label remains unknown.</p>
77	        <figcaption>Read the arrow in its stated direction. An order could be packed without a ready label.</figcaption>
78	      </figure>
79	    </details>}
80	  </section>;
81	}
